Zaleplon: (Major) Reserve concomitant prescribing of buprenorphine and zaleplon for use in patients in whom alternate treatment options are inadequate. Limit dosages and durations to the minimum required and monitor patients closely for respiratory depression and sedation. Gradually tapering a patient off other CNS depressants or decreasing to the lowest effective dose is preferred in most cases of patients being treated for opioid use disorder. If concomitant use is necessary, consider prescribing naloxone for the emergency treatment of opioid overdose. Concomitant use can increase the risk of hypotension, respiratory depression, profound sedation, coma, and death.
Ziconotide: (Moderate) Concurrent use of ziconotide and opiate agonists-antagonists may result in an increased incidence of dizziness and confusion. Ziconotide neither interacts with opiate receptors nor potentiates opiate-induced respiratory depression. However, in animal models, ziconotide did potentiate gastrointestinal motility reduction by opioid agonists.
Zidesamtinib: (Major) Concomitant use of zidesamtinib and buprenorphine increases the risk of QT/QTc prolongation and torsade de pointes (TdP). Avoid concomitant use if possible, especially in patients with additional risk factors for TdP. Consider taking steps to minimize the risk for QT/QTc interval prolongation and TdP, such as electrolyte monitoring and repletion and ECG monitoring, if concomitant use is necessary.
Ziftomenib: (Major) Concomitant use of ziftomenib and buprenorphine increases the risk for QT/QTc prolongation and torsade de pointes (TdP) and may increase buprenorphine exposure, resulting in increased or prolonged opioid effects, particularly when ziftomenib is added after a stable buprenorphine dose has been achieved. Avoid concomitant use if possible, especially in patients with additional risk factors for TdP. If concomitant use is necessary, consider a reduced dose of buprenorphine with frequent monitoring for respiratory depression and sedation. Additionally, consider taking steps to minimize the risk for QT/QTc prolongation and TdP, such as electrolyte monitoring and repletion and ECG monitoring, if concomitant use is necessary. When discontinuing ziftomenib, buprenorphine concentrations may decrease, potentially reducing opioid efficacy or precipitating withdrawal in individuals who have developed physical dependence. If ziftomenib is discontinued, consider increasing the buprenorphine dosage until stable opioid effects are restored and monitor for withdrawal symptoms. Buprenorphine is a CYP3A and CYP2D6 substrate, ziftomenib is a weak CYP3A inhibitor, and both medications have been associated with QT/QTc prolongation.
Ziprasidone: (Major) Avoid the use of buprenorphine and ziprasidone together due to a risk for QT prolongation; additive sedation and CNS depression may also occur. Clinical trial data indicate that ziprasidone causes QT prolongation; there are postmarketing reports of torsade de pointes (TdP) in patients with multiple confounding factors. Buprenorphine has been associated with QT prolongation and has a possible risk of TdP. The prescribing information for some buprenorphine products recommend avoiding use with any drug that has the potential to prolong the QT interval.
ZOLMitriptan: (Moderate) Because of the potential risk and severity of serotonin syndrome, caution should be observed when administering buprenorphine with serotonergic-receptor agonists. Inform patients taking this combination of the possible increased risk and monitor for the emergence of serotonin syndrome particularly during treatment initiation and dose adjustment. Discontinue all serotonergic agents and initiate symptomatic treatment if serotonin syndrome occurs.
Zolpidem: (Major) Reserve concomitant prescribing of buprenorphine and zolpidem for use in patients in whom alternate treatment options are inadequate. Limit dosages and durations to the minimum required and monitor patients closely for respiratory depression and sedation. Limit the dose of Intermezzo sublingual tablets to 1.75 mg/day. Gradually tapering a patient off other CNS depressants or decreasing to the lowest effective dose is preferred in most cases of patients being treated for opioid use disorder. If concomitant use is necessary, consider prescribing naloxone for the emergency treatment of opioid overdose. Concomitant use can increase the risk of hypotension, respiratory depression, profound sedation, coma, and death.
Zuranolone: (Major) Avoid the use of multiple sedating agents due to the risk for additive CNS depression. If use is necessary, consider a downward dosage adjustment of either or both medications, especially in patients with additional risk factors for sedation-related harm.

Adverse Reactions
  Headline
  Adverse Reactions
  General Information
  Abuse of buprenorphine has been reported. In clinical trials, euphoria was reported in less than 1% of the patients. Following chronic administration, patients may experience psychological dependence to buprenorphine. Psychological dependence is a behavioral syndrome characterized by drug craving, overwhelming concern with drug acquisition, and other drug-related behaviors such as drug selling and seeking the drug from multiple sources. Physiological dependence may also occur with buprenorphine; however, since buprenorphine slowly dissociates from the mu-receptor, withdrawal symptoms following abrupt discontinuation are usually mild. Buprenorphine withdrawal symptoms are characteristic of other opioid discontinuations and may persist for 1—2 weeks or be delayed in onset. Withdrawal symptoms may occur when buprenorphine is administered to patients with dependence to full mu-receptor agonists. These symptoms are a result of buprenorphine’s high affinity for the mu-receptor, but less intrinsic activity at this receptor than full mu-receptor agonists. To avoid withdrawal in a patient with opioid dependence, administer the first buprenorphine dose at least 4 hours after the last used opioid or preferably when objective and clear signs of withdrawal occur.
 
The most common adverse reactions reported during a 4-week clinical trial in patients receiving 16 mg/day of sublingual buprenorphine were headache (29.1%), insomnia (21.4%), withdrawal syndrome (18.4%), generalized pain (18.4%), nausea/vomiting (13.6%/7.8%), sweating or diaphoresis (12.6%), infection (11.7%), and abdominal pain (11.7%). Adverse reactions occurring less commonly include rhinitis (9.7%), constipation (7.8%), back pain (7.8%), chills (7.8%), asthenia (4.9%), diarrhea (4.9%), and vasodilation (3.9%). The adverse event profiles in this trial were comparable with patients receiving 16 mg/day of sublingual buprenorphine with naloxone.
 
The most common adverse reactions reported during clinical trials in patients receiving buprenorphine parenterally included sedation or drowsiness (66%), dizziness, vertigo, miosis, headache, nausea, and hypoventilation. Adverse reactions less commonly (< 1% of patients) associated with parenteral buprenorphine were urinary retention, orthostatic hypotension, sinus bradycardia, sinus tachycardia, blurred vision, hypertension, and nervousness or anxiety. Additionally, pallor, cold skin, hypotension, and tachycardia have been reported in a few patients who received buprenorphine epidurally.
 
Respiratory depression may occur following administration of buprenorphine. In adults, the respiratory depressant effect of 0.3 mg buprenorphine given parenterally is similar to the effect obtained with 10 mg morphine. Although the majority of cases are associated with intravenous use, respiratory depression may also occur following administration of sublingual buprenorphine. A reduction of 2—4 breaths/minute from baseline has been observed after sublingual doses of 4—32 mg, with maximal suppression after 16 mg. The respiratory depressant effects of buprenorphine may be increased when used in combination with other depressants (alcohol, benzodiazepines, and opioids); instruct patients to avoid self-administration of depressants while taking buprenorphine. In addition, closely monitor patients with preexisting lung disease while on buprenorphine. as they may be at increased risk of developing respiratory depression. In the event of an overdose, naloxone may not be effective in reversing the respiratory depression produced by buprenorphine. Closely monitor the patient's respiratory and cardiac status and establish a patent airway with institution of mechanical ventilation if needed. If indicated, administer oxygen, intravenous fluids, vasopressors, and other supportive measures. Besides respiratory depression, other signs and symptoms of overdose include cold, clammy skin, confusion, seizures, severe dizziness, drowsiness or weakness, miosis, hypotension, bradycardia, unconsciousness, slow or troubled breathing including dyspnea, and respiratory arrest.
 
Allergic reactions to buprenorphine have occurred and are usually typified by pruritus, urticaria, or rash (unspecified). Although not as common, cases of anaphylactic shock, bronchospasm, and anaphylactoid reactions such as angioedema have been reported.
 
Baseline and periodic liver function tests are recommended in patients receiving buprenorphine. Cases of cytolytic hepatitis, hepatitis with jaundice (hyperbilirubinemia), hepatic failure, hepatic necrosis, and hepatic encephalopathy have been reported in patients treated with buprenorphine. The causality or contributory nature of buprenorphine to these events has not been definitively established. Consider discontinuing buprenorphine in patients who develop hepatotoxicity.
 
Administration of opioids, including buprenorphine, may result in increased intracranial pressure. Use buprenorphine cautiously in patients with head injury, intracranial lesions, or other circumstances where cerebrospinal pressure may be increased. If used in this population, consider buprenorphine as a cause of miosis and decreased consciousness when performing patient evaluations.
 
Buprenorphine may cause CNS depression resulting in impaired cognition and physical ability. When given concurrently with other CNS depressant drugs such as other narcotics, general anesthetics, benzodiazepines, phenothiazines, sedative hypnotics, or tranquilizers the CNS depressant effects may worsen. In patients displaying signs of CNS depression, consider a reduction in the dose of one or both agents.
 
Neonatal withdrawal has occurred in babies born to women treated with buprenorphine during pregnancy. Data from post-marketing reports indicate the time to onset ranges from day 1 to day 8 of life, with the majority of neonates experiencing withdrawal symptoms on day 1. Symptoms associated with neonatal abstinence syndrome include tremor and hyperreflexia, hypertonia, agitation, and myoclonia. In addition, seizures and one case of neonatal respiratory depression with apnea and bradycardia were also reported.
  Adverse Reactions
    Headline
    agitation, anxiety, asthenia, confusion, depression, dizziness, drowsiness, dysarthria, dysgeusia, dysphoria, emotional lability, hallucinations, headache, hypoesthesia, insomnia, memory impairment, migraine, nightmares, paresthesias, psychosis, restlessness, seizures, syncope, tinnitus, tremor, vertigo, weakness
    General Information

The following have been reported during clinical trials with buprenorphine with respective incidence by dosage form (parenteral, sublingual [tablet unless otherwise stated], transdermal, buccal film, subdermal implant, and extended-release subcutaneous injection): agitation (less than 1%, not reported, less than 1%, not reported, not reported, not reported); confusion (less than 1%, not reported, less than 1%, not reported, not reported, not reported); CNS depression (less than 1%, 11% [solution], less than 5%, not reported, 6% subdermal implant, not reported); depersonalization (less than 1%, not reported, less than 1%, not reported, not reported, not reported), dizziness (5 to 10%, 4% [solution], 2 to 15%, 2 to 6%, 4% subdermal implant, not reported); drowsiness or somnolence (not reported, 5% [solution], 2 to 13%, less than 1 to 7%, 3% subdermal implant, 1.5% to 2.5% extended-release subcutaneous injection); Emotional lability, apathy, attention disturbance, dysarthria, dysgeusia, gait disturbance, memory impairment, mental impairment or status changes, nightmares, restlessness, and syncope were reported in less than 1% of patients treated with transdermal buprenorphine. Depression occurred in 1 to 4.9% of patients using the buccal film. Dreaming, slurred speech, dysphoria, and psychosis occurred rarely in less than 1% of those given parenteral buprenorphine. Tinnitus was observed in less than 1% of those given parenteral or transdermal dosage forms. Although not specifically reported during clinical trials, use of buprenorphine in any dosage form may lower seizure threshold and induce seizures in some clinical settings.

    Headline
    coma, impaired cognition
    General Information
    Buprenorphine may cause CNS depression resulting in impaired cognition and physical ability, somnolence, and alterations in levels of consciousness (e.g., coma). Coma has been observed in less than 1% of patients given parenteral buprenorphine during clinical trials. When given concurrently with other CNS depressant drugs, such as other narcotics, general anesthetics, benzodiazepines, phenothiazines, sedative hypnotics, or tranquilizers, the CNS depressant effects may worsen. In patients displaying signs of CNS depression, consider a reduction in the dose of buprenorphine and/or a reduction in the dose of any concurrent CNS depressant.

    Headline
    angina, atrial fibrillation, AV block, bradycardia, chest pain (unspecified), hypertension, hypotension, orthostatic hypotension, pallor, palpitations, peripheral vasodilation, sinus tachycardia
    General Information
    Use of buprenorphine is associated with cardiovascular effects, and patients receiving parenteral or transdermal buprenorphine appear to experience cardiovascular adverse events more often than do those receiving sublingual buprenorphine. In a placebo-controlled trial, no statistically significant differences in blood pressure or heart rate were noted among non-opioid-dependent study patients administered either parenteral buprenorphine at a dose of 2 mg, 4 mg, 8 mg, 12 mg, or 16 mg; sublingual buprenorphine at a dose of 12 mg; or placebo. An elevated systolic blood pressure was noted among those receiving buprenorphine 8 mg IV as compared to those receiving placebo. Hypertension or increased blood pressure has been reported at an incidence of 1 to 4.9% among study patients receiving transdermal or buccal buprenorphine, and in less than 1% of patients receiving parenteral buprenorphine, and has not been reported with sublingual buprenorphine or subdermal implants. Hypotension has been reported in 1 to 5% of patients receiving parenteral buprenorphine, less than 1% of those receiving transdermal buprenorphine, and only in cases of postmarketing overdose among patients receiving sublingual buprenorphine or with other products in overdose; hypotension is a common manifestation of opiate overdose. Buprenorphine may cause severe hypotension in ambulatory patients with depleted blood volume or those who have a reduced ability to maintain blood pressure. Concomitant administration of buprenorphine with phenothiazines or general anesthetics may also cause severe hypotension. Other cardiovascular effects noted during clinical trials of buprenorphine (with incidence and associated dosage form) include: angina pectoris (less than 5% transdermal), atrial fibrillation (0.2% or less buccal), second degree AV block (less than 1% parenteral), bradycardia (less than 1% parenteral and transdermal), chest pain (unspecified) (0.2% or less buccal), coronary artery disease (0.2% or less buccal), orthostatic hypotension (less than 1% transdermal), pallor (less than 1% parenteral), palpitations (less than 1% transdermal), peripheral vasodilation (3.9% sublingual, less than 1% transdermal), sinus tachycardia (less than 1% parenteral, less than 1% transdermal), and elevated blood creatine phosphokinase (3.4% to 4.5% extended-release subcutaneous injection).

    Headline
    QT prolongation
    General Information
    Thorough QT studies with buprenorphine products have demonstrated QT prolongation 15 msec or less. This QTc prolongation effect does not appear to be mediated by hERG channels. Based on these findings, buprenorphine is unlikely to be pro-arrhythmic when used alone in patients without risk factors. The risk of combining buprenorphine with other QT-prolonging agents or in patients at risk for QT prolongation is not known.  In a controlled study of the buprenorphine transdermal system in healthy subjects, a buprenorphine dose of 40 mcg/hour (given as two 20 mcg/hour systems) was observed to cause QT prolongation. Such treated study patients experienced a maximum increase in mean QTc of 9.2 msec (90% CI: 5.2 to13.3). In contrast, buprenorphine 10 mcg/hour did not demonstrate a clinically meaningful effect on the QT interval. Doses of transdermal buprenorphine should not exceed 20 mcg/hour due to an increased risk for the development of QT prolongation. During clinical trials with buccal buprenorphine, QT prolongation occurred in less than 1% of patients. The dosage of buccal buprenorphine should not exceed 900 mcg twice daily due to the risk of QT prolongation. It is possible that other dosage forms may also rarely cause QT prolongation. In a clinical trial of patients receiving buprenorphine, levomethadyl, or methadone for treatment of opioid dependence, buprenorphine use was associated with less effect on QT interval than either levomethadyl or methadone use; an absolute QTc value greater than 500 milliseconds occurred in 0 of 54 patients receiving buprenorphine, 6 of 52 patients receiving methadone, and 5 of 46 patients receiving levomethadyl during the 17-week trial. The odds ratio of a QTc change of more than 60 milliseconds, considered a clinically significant change, was calculated as 15.8 with the use of levomethadyl vs. buprenorphine (95% CI: 3.7 to 67.1) and as 8.4 with the use of methadone versus buprenorphine (95% CI: 1.9 to 36.4). According to the investigators, study patients receiving buprenorphine rarely experienced a QTc change greater than 60 milliseconds.

    abdominal pain, anorexia, constipation, dehydration, diarrhea, dyspepsia, dysphagia, flatulence, hiccups, ileus, nausea, vomiting, weight loss, xerostomia
    General Information
    Adverse gastrointestinal effects may occur with buprenorphine use. The following have been reported during clinical trials with buprenorphine with respective incidence by dosage form (parenteral, sublingual [tablet unless otherwise stated], transdermal, buccal film, subdermal implant, and extended-release subcutaneous injection): abdominal pain (11.7%, not reported, 1 to 5%, 1 to 4.9%, 3%, not reported), anorexia (less than 1%, not reported, 1 to 5%, 1 to 4.9%, not reported, not reported), constipation (less than 1%, 7.8%, 3 to 13%, 4 to 11%, 6%, 8% to 9.4%), diarrhea (less than 1%, 4.9%, 1 to 5%, 5% or more, not reported, not reported), dyspepsia (less than 1%, 3% [solution], 1 to 5%, less than 1%, not reported, not reported), flatulence (less than 1%, not reported, less than 1%, not reported, 1%, not reported), nausea (5 to 10%, 13.6%, 6 to 23%, 9 to 33%, 6%, 8% to 8.9%), and vomiting (1 to 5%, 7.8%, 4 to 9%, 5 to 7%, 6%, 5.5% to 9.4%). Abdominal distension, dehydration, diverticulitis, dysphagia, hiccups, ileus, and weight loss occurred in less than 1% of patients during clinical trials of transdermal buprenorphine. Ileus and dehydration were reported in 0.2% or less of patients treated with buccal buprenorphine. Xerostomia was reported in less than 1%, 6%, and 5% or more of patients treated with buprenorphine injection, transdermal patch, and buccal film, respectively. Abdominal discomfort was reported in less than 1% of patients treated with the buccal film.

    Headline
    apnea, cough, cyanosis, dyspnea, hyperventilation, hypoventilation, hypoxia, respiratory depression, wheezing
    General Information
    As with the use of other opioid agonists, clinically significant respiratory depression may occur following administration of therapeutic doses of buprenorphine. In adults, the respiratory depressant effect of buprenorphine 0.3 mg given parenterally is similar to the effect obtained with morphine 10 mg. Single doses of sublingual buprenorphine solution at a dose of 4, 8, 16, and 32 mg resulted in a greater incidence of hypoventilation not requiring medical intervention than did single doses of oral methadone at doses of 15, 30, 45, and 60 mg. In clinical trials with transdermal buprenorphine, respiratory depression was reported in less than 1% of patients. Dyspnea has been reported in less than 1% of patients receiving parenteral buprenorphine, 1 to 4.9% of patients receiving transdermal buprenorphine, less than 1% of patients receiving buccal buprenorphine, 1% of patients receiving the subdermal implant, and was not specifically reported in patients receiving sublingual buprenorphine. Other noted respiratory effects with the respective incidence and associated dosage form include apnea (less than 1% parenteral), cough or cough increase (4% with sublingual, 1 to 4.9% transdermal, less than 1% buccal, 3% subdermal implant), cyanosis (less than 1% parenteral), hyperventilation (less than 1% transdermal), and wheezing or asthma aggravation (less than 1% transdermal).The respiratory depressant effects of buprenorphine may be increased when used in combination with other CNS/respiratory depressants (e.g., alcohol, benzodiazepines, and other opioids); instruct patients to avoid self-administration of such depressants while taking buprenorphine. Deaths have been reported in patients who misuse buprenorphine and/or use buprenorphine in combination with other respiratory depressants. Buprenorphine-induced respiratory depression may not be fully reversed by the administration of naloxone in the event of an overdose; supportive measures may be needed to maintain adequate ventilation. Patients with preexisting lung disease may be at increased risk of developing respiratory depression. Opioids can cause sleep-related breathing disorders including central sleep apnea (CSA) and sleep-related hypoxemia (hypoxia). Opioid use increases the risk of CSA in a dose-dependent fashion. In patients who present with CSA, consider decreasing the opioid dosage using best practices for opioid taper.

    Headline
    anaphylactic shock, anaphylactoid reactions, angioedema, bronchospasm, ecchymosis, edema, erythema, hematoma, injection site reaction, pruritus, rash, skin irritation, urticaria
    General Information
    Allergic reactions to buprenorphine have occurred during clinical trials and the postmarketing period. Although not common, cases of anaphylactic shock, bronchospasm, and anaphylactoid reactions such as angioedema (less than 1%) have been reported. During clinical trials, pruritus, urticaria, or rash (unspecified) were observed in less than 5%, less than 1%, and 1 to 4.9% of patients who received parenteral or transdermal buprenorphine, respectively. Pruritus and rash were reported in 1 to 4.9% of patients treated with buccal buprenorphine. Excoriation was reported in less than 1% of patients treated with buccal buprenorphine and 2% of patients treated with subdermal implants in clinical trials. In addition, application site reactions have been reported with the buprenorphine transdermal system. Rarely, severe skin reactions have occurred with signs of marked inflammation, including discharge and vesicles. Application site pruritus (4 to 15%), rash (3 to 8%), erythema (3 to 10%), and skin irritation (1 to 6%) have been reported with the transdermal system. The time of onset varies from days to months after the initiation of treatment. Patients should report the development of severe application site reactions and discontinue treatment with the buprenorphine transdermal system. An injection site reaction has rarely been observed (less than 1%) with parenteral buprenorphine use. During clinical trial evaluation of the subdermal implant (Probuphine), the following implant site-related adverse events were reported in 2% or more of patients receiving active drug: any implant site treatment emergent adverse event (TEAE) (37%), implant site pain (13%), implant site pruritus (12%), implant site erythema (10%), implant site hematoma (7%), implant site hemorrhage (7%), implant site edema (5%). Additional dermatologic effects that occurred during clinical trial evaluation of the subdermal implants in buprenorphine-treated patients included rash (2%) and skin lesion (1%). The overall incidence of injection site reactions occurring during premarketing evaluation of the extended-release subcutaneous injections (Sublocade and Brixadi, respectively) included any injection site reaction (16.5% and 20.7%), injection site pain (7.2% and 9.9%), pruritus (6.6% and 6.1%), erythema (4.7% and 6.6%), induration (1.4%), ecchymosis / bruising (0.7%), swelling (0.7% and 4.7%), discomfort (0.6%), cellulitis (0.4%), and infection (0.4%). With the extended-release subcutaneous injection, prescribers should regularly examine the injection site for signs of infection or evidence of tampering or attempts to remove the subcutaneous depot.

    Headline
    cholecystitis, elevated hepatic enzymes, hepatic encephalopathy, hepatic failure, hepatic necrosis, hepatitis, hyperbilirubinemia, jaundice
    General Information
    Baseline and periodic liver function tests are recommended in patients receiving sublingual buprenorphine or buprenorphine subdermal implants and in patients receiving transdermal or buccal buprenorphine who are at increased risk of hepatotoxicity (e.g., those with history of excess alcohol or intravenous drug use and those with pre-existing liver disease). Cases of cytolytic hepatitis, hepatitis with jaundice (hyperbilirubinemia), hepatic failure, hepatic necrosis, hepatorenal syndrome, and hepatic encephalopathy have been reported in patients treated with sublingual buprenorphine or transdermal buprenorphine, and may be possible in those treated with other dosage forms of the medication. Cases of elevated hepatic enzymes (5% or less) and increased gamma-glutamyl transferase (GGT) (3% to 4%) have been observed. The causality or contributory nature of buprenorphine to these events has not been definitively established. Cholecystitis was reported in 0.2% or less of patients treated with buccal buprenorphine during clinical trials. Consider discontinuing buprenorphine in patients who develop hepatotoxicity.

    Headline
    amenorrhea, gonadal suppression, hyponatremia, impotence (erectile dysfunction), infertility, libido decrease, SIADH, urinary incontinence, urinary retention
    General Information
    Genitourinary effects including libido decrease (less than 1% transdermal), unspecified sexual dysfunction (less than 1% transdermal), urinary retention (less than 1% parenteral and transdermal), urinary hesitation (less than 1% transdermal), and urinary incontinence (less than 1% transdermal) have been reported with buprenorphine products. Opioid agonists can interfere with the endocrine system by inhibiting the secretion of adrenocorticotropic hormone (ACTH), cortisol, and luteinizing hormone (LH), and by stimulating secretion of prolactin, growth hormone (GH), insulin, and glucagon. Chronic opioid use may influence the hypothalamic-pituitary-gonadal axis, leading to hormonal changes that may manifest as hypogonadism (gonadal suppression). Although the exact causal role of opioids in the clinical manifestations of hypogonadism is unknown, patients could experience libido decrease, impotence (erectile dysfunction), amenorrhea, or infertility. Other various medical, physical, lifestyle, and psychological stressors may influence gonadal hormone concentrations; these stressors have not been adequately controlled for in clinical studies with opioids. Patients presenting with signs or symptoms of androgen deficiency should undergo laboratory evaluation. Opioid agonists can inhibit the release of thyrotropin, leading to a decrease in thyroid hormone. Opioid drugs may also stimulate the release of vasopressin (ADH); hyponatremia can occur as a result of SIADH.

    Headline
    adrenocortical insufficiency
    General Information
    Opioids may interfere with the endocrine system by inhibiting the secretion of adrenocorticotropic hormone (ACTH) and cortisol. Rarely, adrenocortical insufficiency has been reported in association with opioid use, more often following greater than 1 month of use. Patients should seek immediate medical attention if they experience symptoms such as nausea, vomiting, loss of appetite, fatigue, weakness, dizziness, or hypotension. If adrenocortical insufficiency is suspected, confirm with diagnostic testing as soon as possible. If diagnosed, the patient should be treated with physiologic replacement doses of corticosteroids, and if appropriate, weaned off of opioid therapy. If the opioid can be discontinued, a follow-up assessment of adrenal function should be performed to determine if corticosteroid treatment can be discontinued. Other opioids may be tried; some cases reported use of a different opioid with no recurrence of adrenocortical insufficiency. It is unclear which, if any, opioids are more likely to cause adrenocortical insufficiency.
    Adverse Reaction GSTerms
      GSTerm Name
      adrenocortical insufficiency
      Severity
      Moderate
    Headline
    euphoria, physiological dependence, psychological dependence, withdrawal
    General Information
    Euphoria is not a common side effect of buprenorphine; incidences of 0% to 1% have been reported, depending on the product used. As with other opioids, buprenorphine presents the potential for abuse or psychological dependence. Some dosage forms, like the subdermal implants or extended-release subcutaneous injections, are formulated to deter illicit use, and are only administered via healthcare professional access. Although the true prevalence of addiction to opioids in chronic pain patients is unknown, estimates from multiple studies indicate that it may range from 0% to 50% and 0% to 7.7% in chronic nonmalignant pain and cancer pain, respectively. Physiological dependence, however, will occur during chronic opiate agonist therapy as evidenced by a withdrawal syndrome occurring after abrupt discontinuation of the drug in these patients. Withdrawal was reported in 18.4% to 24% of those using sublingual tablets, less than 1% of those using transdermal buprenorphine, and 2% to 3% of those using buccal buprenorphine. Physiological dependence may occur with buprenorphine; however, since buprenorphine slowly dissociates from the mu-receptor, withdrawal symptoms following abrupt discontinuation are usually mild and may be delayed in onset. Common withdrawal symptoms include restlessness, lacrimation, rhinorrhea, yawning, perspiration, chills, myalgia, and mydriasis. Irritability, anxiety, backache, joint pain, weakness, abdominal cramps, insomnia, nausea, anorexia, vomiting, diarrhea, and increased blood pressure, respiratory rate, or heart rate may also occur. Withdrawal symptoms may also occur when buprenorphine is administered to patients with opioid dependence to full mu-receptor agonists, as buprenorphine exhibits less intrinsic activity at the mu-receptor than full mu-receptor agonists. To lessen undue symptoms of withdrawal when initiating treatment of opioid dependence, administer the first sublingual/transmucosal buprenorphine dose at least 4 hours after the last used opioid or preferably when objective and clear signs of withdrawal from the addiction first occur.

    Headline
    neonatal opioid withdrawal syndrome, neonatal respiratory depression
    General Information
    Routine use of opioid agonists, such as buprenorphine, by an expectant mother can lead to depressed respiration in the newborn and a neonatal opioid withdrawal syndrome. Neonatal opioid withdrawal syndrome is estimated to occur in up to 50% of neonates born to opioid-dependent mothers. Withdrawal symptoms may include irritability, hyperactivity, abnormal sleep pattern, tremor, high-pitched crying, vomiting, diarrhea, poor feeding, failure to gain weight, rigidity, and seizures. Onset, duration, and severity of opioid withdrawal may vary based on the specific opioid used, duration of use, timing and amount of last maternal use, and rate of elimination by the newborn. Monitor neonates exposed to buprenorphine closely as neonatal opioid withdrawal can be life-threatening if not recognized and treated. Severe symptoms may require pharmacologic therapy managed by clinicians familiar with neonatal opioid withdrawal. Data from postmarketing reports for buprenorphine sublingual indicate the time to onset ranges from day 1 to day 8 of life, with the majority of neonates experiencing withdrawal symptoms on day 1. Seizures and neonatal respiratory depression with apnea and bradycardia have also been observed.

    Headline
    serotonin syndrome
    General Information
    Serotonin syndrome has been reported in patients taking opioids with serotonergic drugs at recommended doses. Patients taking an opioid concomitantly with a serotonergic medication should seek immediate medical attention if they develop symptoms such as agitation, hallucinations, tachycardia, fever, excessive sweating, shivering or shaking, muscle twitching or stiffness, trouble with coordination, nausea, vomiting, or diarrhea. Symptoms generally present within hours to days of taking an opioid with another serotonergic agent, but may also occur later, particularly after a dosage increase. If serotonin syndrome is suspected, discontinue the serotonergic agents and institute appropriate treatment.

    Headline
    dental caries, dental pain, glossitis, oral ulceration, stomatitis
    General Information
    Oral adverse events reported postmarketing with transmucosal dosage forms of buprenorphine have included glossodynia, glossitis, oral mucosal erythema, oral hypoesthesia, and stomatitis or oral ulceration. The risk of dental disease is increased in patients using transmucosal products containing buprenorphine. A total of 305 cases of dental adverse events have been reported with transmucosal buprenorphine use with a median time to diagnosis of 24.25 months (range 0.5 to 182 months). Tooth decay, dental caries, dental pain, oral dental abscesses and infections, tooth erosion, filling loss, and tooth loss have been reported, even in patients with no history of dental issues. Combinations of rampant dental decay, tooth loss, and tooth fractures across multiple teeth were reported, with 113 cases involving 2 or more teeth. While most adverse events occurred in patients using transmucosal buprenorphine for the treatment of opioid use disorder, 28 patients using transmucosal buprenorphine for an indication of pain experienced adverse dental outcomes. Twenty-six patients reported no prior history of dental problems. The most common treatment was tooth extraction. Other cases reported requiring root canals, dental surgery, and other procedures such as crowns and implants. Refer all patients to dental care services and encourage them to receive consistent dental care while using transmucosal buprenorphine products. The benefits of buprenorphine treatment clearly outweigh the risks of dental problems in patients with opioid use disorder. Patients may lessen the risk of dental problems by swishing and swallowing water immediately after the medicine is completely dissolved. Patients should wait 1 hour before brushing teeth following medication administration to avoid tooth damage. Dentists should perform a baseline dental evaluation and caries risk assessment, establish a dental caries preventive plan, and encourage regular dental checkups in patients taking transmucosal buprenorphine products.

    Headline
    hyperalgesia
    General Information
    Cases of opioid-induced hyperalgesia (OIH) have been reported, both with short-term and longer-term use of opioids. OIH occurs when an opioid paradoxically causes an increase in pain or an increase in sensitivity to pain. Symptoms of OIH include increased levels of pain upon opioid dosage increase, decreased levels of pain upon opioid dosage decrease, or pain from ordinarily non-painful stimuli (allodynia). These symptoms may suggest OIH only if there is no evidence of underlying disease progression, opioid tolerance, opioid withdrawal, or addictive behavior. Data suggests a strong biologic plausibility between opioids and OIH and allodynia. If OIH is suspected, carefully consider appropriately decreasing the dose of the current opioid analgesic or opioid rotation (safely switching to a different opioid).

Description
  Text
  Buprenorphine is a semisynthetic mixed opiate agonist-antagonist. Buprenorphine, a partial mu-receptor agonist, exhibits a ceiling to its pharmacological effects; thus, the danger of overdose, abuse liability, and toxicity from buprenorphine may be less than with full opioid agonists. The immediate-release parenteral formulation of buprenorphine was first approved by the FDA in 1981 and is used for the relief of moderate to severe pain; subsequent FDA approvals of the transdermal and buccal formulations are used for the management of chronic severe pain in patients who require daily, around-the-clock, long-term opioid treatment. A sublingual formulation (e.g., Subutex), a 6-month subdermal implant (Probuphine), and extended-release subcutaneous injections (Sublocade or Brixadi) are used for the treatment of opioid dependence. Use of buprenorphine in-office treatment of opioid dependence was initially restricted to providers with a unique identification number (known as an X-waiver) under the federal Drug Addiction Treatment Act (DATA). However, these restrictions were lifted in 2023 with the passage of the Consolidated Appropriations act, allowing all practitioners who are eligible to prescribe DEA schedule III medications with a current DEA registration to prescribe buprenorphine products approved for the treatment of opiate dependence. In a study, comparable reductions in illicit opioid usage were found with 3 treatments for opioid dependence: buprenorphine, methadone, and levomethadyl acetate. The dosages were individually optimized within a range of 16 to 48 mg 3 times a week for buprenorphine, 60 to 100 mg daily for methadone, and 75 to 161 mg 3 times a week for levomethadyl acetate.

Mechanism Of Action
  Text
  Buprenorphine is a mixed agonist-antagonist narcotic. It acts as a partial agonist at the mu-opioid receptor, an antagonist at the kappa-opioid receptor, an agonist at the delta-opioid receptor, and a partial agonist at the ORL-1 (nociceptin/orphanin FQ) receptor. Opiate agonists are believed to exert their effects by stimulating specific opiate receptors, designated as mu, kappa, delta, and ORL-1 which have been reclassified by an International Union of Pharmacology subcommittee as DOP (delta), KOP (kappa), MOP (mu), and NOP (ORL-1). Mu-receptors, where buprenorphine is an agonist, are considered the classic morphine-receptor type, as stimulation at this receptor produces supraspinal analgesia, respiratory depression, euphoria, and physical dependence. Actions at kappa-receptors, where buprenorphine is an antagonist, are believed to produce alterations in the perception of pain and the emotional response to pain. Dysphoria is often experienced from kappa-receptor activation. It is hypothesized that patients with opioid or cocaine drug dependence have increased kappa-receptor activity to modulate the mu-receptor agonistic effects. Patients with dependence to full opioid agonists are more likely to experience euthymia following administration of buprenorphine, which increases mu-receptor activity and blocks kappa-receptor activity, as compared to following administration of a pure mu-receptor antagonist (i.e., naloxone or naltrexone), which block mu-receptors while leaving the full agonist effects of kappa-receptor activation unopposed. The effect of buprenorphine activity at delta and ORL-1 (NOP) receptors is not fully elucidated. Buprenorphine binds mu-receptors with high affinity; however, buprenorphine has less intrinsic activity at mu-receptors than do full opioid agonists. Blockade of the effects of full mu-receptor agonists occurs after acute administration of buprenorphine. Buprenorphine very slowly dissociates from the mu-receptor, which likely accounts for its longer duration of action than morphine, the unpredictability of its reversal by opioid antagonists, and its low level of manifested physical dependence. Partial agonist typically have a ceiling effect on both respiratory depression and analgesic effect; the analgesic ceiling effect has been demonstrated with sublingual buprenorphine.
 
The pharmacologic effects observed after opiates bind to their receptors involve a second messenger such as cyclic AMP, which is synthesized by adenylate cyclase. Opioid receptors are coupled to these second messenger systems through an inhibitory G-protein (guanine nucleotide-binding protein). G-proteins are located at the cell surface along with many other receptors, including opioid receptors. G-proteins are thought to interact with opiate receptors, giving the receptor a higher affinity for the opiate. Binding of the opiate stimulates the exchange of guanosine triphosphate (GTP) for guanosine diphosphate (GDP) on the G-protein complex. Binding of GTP leads to a release of the G-protein subunit, which acts on the effector system. In this case, the effector system is adenylate cyclase and cyclic AMP located at the inner surface of the plasma membrane. Opioid agonists effectively inhibit adenylate cyclase and cause a decrease in intracellular cyclic AMP levels. Other research has shown that mu-, delta-, and kappa-receptors are associated with ion channels and control the influx of cations into the cell. Mu- and delta-receptor stimulation is associated with increasing potassium influx and kappa-receptor activity is associated with reducing calcium influx in cells located in various human and animal nerve systems. All of these effects appear to ultimately reduce transmitter release and may also be mediated through G-proteins.

Pharmacokinetics
  Route Specific Pharmacokinetics
    Name
    Oral Route
    Description
    Sublingual Route (e.g., Subutex and generic equivalents): Buprenorphine displays poor oral bioavailability; however, bioavailability improves with sublingual administration. Plasma concentrations (Cmax) and drug exposure (AUC) of buprenorphine increase linearly, though not in direct dose proportionality, with increasing sublingual dose over a range of 4 to 16 mg. Opioid agonist effects of sublingual buprenorphine are limited by a ceiling effect which occurs between 8 and 16 mg; doses above this maximum produced no additional effect. The mean elimination half-life of sublingually administered buprenorphine is 37 hours.
 
Transmucosal Route (Belbuca): The absolute bioavailability of buprenorphine with transmucosal administration ranges from 46 to 65%. With multiple dose administration, steady-state plasma concentrations were achieved prior to the sixth dose. Steady-state Cmax and AUC increase proportionally to dose. Administration of the film with cold, hot, or room temperature water reduced systemic buprenorphine exposure by 23 to 27%. Low pH liquids such as decaffeinated cola decreased drug exposure by 37%.
    Name
    Intravenous Route
    Description
    The onset of action and time to peak effect of buprenorphine are more rapid with IV administration as compared to IM or epidural administration. The elimination half-life of intravenous buprenorphine (0.3 mg IV) is 1.2 to 7.2 hours with an average of 2.2 hours.
    Name
    Intramuscular Route
    Description
    Pharmacological effects of buprenorphine occur as soon as 15 minutes after IM injection and usually last for 6 to 10 hours. Peak pharmacologic effects usually are observed at 1 hour. As expected, time to onset of action and peak effect are shorter with IV administration.
    Name
    Subcutaneous Route
    Description
    Subcutaneous depot extended-release injection (Sublocade): After subcutaneous injection of this product, the median time to maximum concentration (median Tmax) occurred at 24 hours post-dose. The product is injected subcutaneously as a liquid, with subsequent precipitation into a solid depot that contains buprenorphine. Buprenorphine is then released via diffusion from the biodegradation of the depot. Steady-state is reached in 4 to 6 months. The steady-state concentrations of the major metabolite, norbuprenorphine, are low compared to buprenorphine. The terminal half-life of buprenorphine extended-release subcutaneous injection has a range of 43 to 60 days due to slow release from the subcutaneous depot. Patients who discontinue the drug may have detectable plasma and urine concentrations of buprenorphine for 12 months or longer. Therefore, patients who discontinue treatment should be monitored for several months for signs and symptoms of withdrawal and treated appropriately. Buprenorphine may be detected in plasma and urine 22 to 38 months following last exposure, and it is expected that buprenorphine will be detected in patients for a longer time in urine than in plasma. Mean buprenorphine plasma concentrations for Cavg, Cmax, and Ctrough at steady-state for this product have been compared to daily transmucosal buprenorphine; consult the manufacturer literature for comparison tables.
Subcutaneous extended-release injection (Brixadi): Following subcutaneous injection, the extended-release formulation transforms from a low viscous solution to a liquid crystalline gel that encapsulates buprenorphine and releases it at a steady rate as the depot biodegrades. After administration, the buprenorphine plasma concentration increases with a median Tmax of about 24 hours for the weekly formulation and 6 to 10 hours for the monthly formulation. Based on trough levels after each dose, steady-state exposure is reached at administration of the fourth weekly or monthly dose. Norbuprenorphine steady-state plasma concentrations after subcutaneous injection of the weekly or monthly formulations are low compared to buprenorphine (AUC norbuprenorphine/buprenorphine ratio of 0.35). After multiple dose subcutaneous injections of the 32 mg/week buprenorphine product at different injection sites (abdomen, thigh, buttock, or upper arm), a comparable PK exposure was observed. However, injection into the upper arm was associated with approximately 10% lower plasma levels than other sites. The terminal plasma half-life of the subcutaneous buprenorphine formulation ranged from 3 to 5 days for the weekly injection and 19 to 26 days for the monthly injection. Mean buprenorphine plasma concentrations for Cavg, Cmax, and Ctrough at steady-state for this product have been compared to daily transmucosal buprenorphine; consult the manufacturer literature for comparison tables.
    Name
    Topical Route
    Description
    Transdermal Route (Butrans)
The proportion of buprenorphine mixed in the adhesive matrix is the same in each of the three different strengths of the buprenorphine transdermal system. The amount of buprenorphine released from the system per hour is proportional to the active surface area of the system. The 5, 10, and 20 mcg/hour patches provide dose-proportional buprenorphine AUCs after 7-day applications. Additionally, the pharmacokinetic profile is similar when applied to each of the suggested application sites; the skin is the limiting barrier to diffusion from the system into the bloodstream. Data from clinical studies suggest that the median time for the buprenorphine 10 mcg/hour transdermal system to deliver quantifiable buprenorphine concentrations (>= 25 pg/mL) is approximately 17 hours. The bioavailability relative to intravenous administration after a 7-day application is approximately 15% for all doses. Application of a heating pad directly to the buprenorphine transdermal system increased buprenorphine blood concentrations by 26 to 55%. Concentrations returned to normal within 5 hours after the heat was removed. Similarly, fever may increase the permeability of the skin and lead to increased buprenorphine concentrations; however, in a crossover study of healthy subjects, the AUC and Cmax did not change significantly in patients with a mild fever. A minimum rest period of 21 days is required before reapplication to the same site, in order to avoid variability in drug absorption. Steady state is achieved by day 3 after application. After dose titration, plasma buprenorphine concentrations show no further change over a 60-day study period. Buprenorphine metabolism in the skin after transdermal application is negligible. After removal of the system, mean buprenorphine concentrations decreased by 50% within 10 to 24 hours. The terminal half-life is approximately 26 hours.
    Name
    Other Route(s)
    Description
    Epidural Route
The duration of epidurally administered buprenorphine analgesic effect appears to be dose-dependent. In a double-blind controlled trial (n = 158), postoperative analgesia, as measured by patient report every 3 hours, was greater than placebo for 6 hours in patients administered 0.15 mg buprenorphine at the end of surgery (p less than 0.05) and for 12 hours in patients administered 0.3 mg buprenorphine at the end of surgery (p less than 0.05); no statistically significant differences in adverse events were reported between the groups.
  Special Populations
    Name
    Hepatic Impairment
    Description
    In a pharmacokinetic study, the mean Cmax of sublingual buprenorphine increased by 72%, the mean AUC increased by 181%, and the mean half-life was prolonged 57% in patients with severe hepatic impairment compared to healthy controls. In patients with moderate hepatic impairment receiving sublingual buprenorphine, the mean Cmax increased by 8%, the mean AUC increased by 64%, and the mean half-life was prolonged 35% compared to healthy controls. In subjects with mild hepatic impairment, there were no clinically significant changes in mean Cmax, AUC, and half-life compared to healthy controls. Dosage adjustments are recommended in hepatic impairment for some products based upon the severity of the hepatic impairment and the buprenorphine formulation being administered. The effects of hepatic impairment on the kinetics of extended-release buprenorphine injections (Sublocade or Brixadi) have not been studied; however, based on data from the sublingual tablets, these products are not recommended in patients with moderate to severe hepatic impairment.
    Name
    Renal Impairment
    Description
    No differences in buprenorphine pharmacokinetics were observed between 9 dialysis-dependent and 6 normal patients following intravenous administration of 0.3 mg buprenorphine. Further, patients with normal renal function, impaired renal function, and those with renal failure, including those on dialysis, were found to have similar plasma buprenorphine concentrations following IV bolus and IV infusion administrations. The effects of renal impairment on the kinetics of extended-release buprenorphine injections (Sublocade or Brixadi) have not been studied and clinical studies of these products did not include subjects with severe renal impairment.
    Name
    Pediatrics
    Description
    Clearance of buprenorphine immediate-release injection may be faster in some children aged 2 to 12 years than in adults. A dosage interval of 4 to 5 hours may be appropriate in some pediatric patients (versus 6 to 8 hours in adults). However, there is some evidence that not all children clear buprenorphine faster than adults; some pediatric patients may not need to be remedicated for 6 to 8 hours. The safety and efficacy and pharmacokinetic parameters of other dosage forms have not been established in children.
    Name
    Geriatric
    Description
    Based on population pharmacokinetic analyses, age does not have a clinically meaningful effect on the kinetics of extended-release subcutaneous buprenorphine injections.
    Name
    Gender Differences
    Description
    Based on population pharmacokinetic analyses, gender does not have a clinically meaningful effect on the kinetics of extended-release subcutaneous buprenorphine injections.
    Name
    Ethnic Differences
    Description
    Based on population pharmacokinetic analyses, ethnicity does not have a clinically meaningful effect on the kinetics of extended-release subcutaneous buprenorphine injections.
    Name
    Other
    Description
    Oral Mucositis
In cancer patients with grade 3 mucositis (n = 6), buprenorphine was absorbed more rapidly from the buccal film compared to healthy controls, resulting in approximately 79% higher Cmax and 56% higher AUC.
  Text
  Buprenorphine is administered orally via the sublingual or buccal route, parenterally by intravenous (IV), intramuscular (IM), or epidural injection, and via extended-release subcutaneous injectable formulations. Within the systemic circulation, buprenorphine is approximately 96% protein bound, primarily to alpha and beta globulin. The drug undergoes N-dealkylation metabolism by CYP3A4 isozymes to an active metabolite, norbuprenorphine. A portion of the norbuprenorphine then undergoes further glucuronidation metabolism. The clearance of buprenorphine is related to hepatic blood flow; when administered with drugs that decrease this flow (e.g., some general anesthetics) buprenorphine elimination may be impaired. As determined in a pharmacokinetic study involving radiolabeled drug, buprenorphine is primarily eliminated in the feces (69%) with 31% eliminated in the urine; collected compounds included buprenorphine, norbuprenorphine, and 2 unidentified buprenorphine metabolites.
 
Affected cytochrome P450 isoenzymes and drug transporters: CYP3A4
Buprenorphine is metabolized to its major metabolite, norbuprenorphine, primarily by CYP3A4. Based on studies using transmucosal buprenorphine, patients receiving concomitant treatment with CYP3A4 inhibitors (e.g., ketoconazole, protease inhibitors, macrolide antibiotics) or CYP3A4 inducers (e.g., phenobarbital, phenytoin, carbamazepine, rifampicin) should be monitored for alterations in buprenorphine plasma concentrations, regardless of the product administered. Buprenorphine has been shown to be a CYP3A4 and CYP2D6 inhibitor and in vitro studies have shown that norbuprenorphine is a modest CYP2D6 inhibitor; however, clinically relevant interactions of buprenorphine with CYP2D6 substrates are not expected.

Administration
  General Information
  For storage information, see specific product information within the How Supplied section.
  Route Specific Administrations
    Administration Details
      Name
      Oral Solid Formulations
      Description
      The risk for dental disease may be increased in people treated with sublingual buprenorphine. Encourage treated individuals to receive consistent dental care and checkups while using these products.
The risk of dental problems may be lessened by swishing and swallowing water immediately after a dose is completely administered and dissolved. Patients should wait 1 hour before brushing teeth to avoid tooth damage.
 
Sublingual tablets (e.g., Subutex)
Completely dissolve under the tongue. Do not swallow or chew the tablets; swallowing the tablet will reduce the bioavailability of the drug.
For doses requiring more than 2 tablets, patients are advised to either place all the tablets at once under the tongue or, if they cannot fit more than 2 tablets comfortably, place 2 tablets at a time under the tongue. With either option, patients should hold the tablets under the tongue until they completely dissolve.
Prior to induction, determine the type of opioid dependence (i.e., long- or short-acting opioids).
Induction: To avoid precipitating withdrawal, induction with buprenorphine should be undertaken only after objective and clear signs of withdrawal are present.
Short-acting opioid: Administer first dose at least 4 hours or longer after the patient last used heroin or other short-acting opioid.
Longer-acting opioid (e.g., methadone): Administer first dose at least 24 hours or longer after the patient last long-acting opioid use.
Maintenance: Buprenorphine; naloxone combination products are preferred to buprenorphine single-ingredient products for maintenance treatment of OUD.
Storage: Keep buprenorphine secured in a location not accessible by others.
Disposal: Flush unused buprenorphine down the toilet when it is no longer needed if a drug take-back option is not readily available.
      Name
      Other Oral Formulations
      Description
      The risk for dental disease may be increased in people treated with buccal buprenorphine. Encourage treated individuals to receive consistent dental care and checkups while using these products.
The risk of dental problems may be lessened by swishing and swallowing water immediately after a dose is completely administered and dissolved. Patients should wait 1 hour before brushing teeth to avoid tooth damage.
 
Buccal film (e.g., Belbuca)
Do not use if the package seal is broken or the film is cut, damaged, or changed in any way.
Use the tongue to wet the inside of the check or rinse the mouth with water to wet the area for placement.
Avoid applying the film to areas of the mouth with open sores or lesions.
Apply the film immediately after removal from the package.
Place the yellow side of the film against the inside of the cheek. Hold the film in place with clean, dry fingers for 5 seconds. Leave the film in place until it fully dissolves, usually within 30 minutes.
Do not manipulate the film with the tongue or fingers after it is in place. Avoid eating food and drinking liquids until the film has dissolved. Do not chew or swallow the film.
Abuse or misuse of the film by swallowing may cause choking, overdose, and death.
Storage: Keep buprenorphine secured in a location not accessible by others.
Disposal: Remove film from the foil packaging and flush unused medication down the toilet if a drug take-back option is not readily available.
      Name
      Extemporaneous Compounding-Oral
      Description
      Extemporaneous compounding instructions for 0.075 mg/mL sublingual buprenorphine solution
NOTE: Buprenorphine sublingual solution is not approved by the FDA.
Open a 0.3 mg/mL ampule of buprenorphine.
Add 1.26 mL of 95% ethanol to 0.3 mg buprenorphine (1 mL of the 0.3 mg/mL ampule solution).
Add simple syrup to obtain a final total volume of 4 mL.
Storage: The solution is stable in amber glass bottles (for 30 days) and oral dispensing syringes (for 7 days) when stored at room temperature at 68 to 77 degrees F (20 to 25 degrees C).
Administration
Using an oral syringe, place under the tongue for sublingual administration.
A pacifier may be placed in the neonate's mouth to maximize contact with the sublingual mucosa.
If the volume is more than 0.5 mL, divide into 2 administrations separated by at least 2 minutes.
    Name
    Oral Administration
    Administration Details
      Name
      Intravenous Administration
      Description
      Immediate-release injection solution (e.g., Buprenex)
No dilution is necessary. Visually inspect parenteral products for particulate matter and discoloration before administration whenever solution and container permit.
Give by slow IV injection over 2 minutes directly into a vein or into the tubing of a freely flowing, compatible IV solution. Rapid IV injection may result in an increased frequency of adverse effects.
Do not exceed the maximum of 0.3 mg/dose IV. If an adult patient (not in a high-risk category) requires a single dose more than 0.3 mg, then administer via the intramuscular route only.
      Name
      Intramuscular Administration
      Description
      Immediate-release injection solution (e.g., Buprenex)
No dilution is necessary. Visually inspect parenteral products for particulate matter and discoloration before administration whenever solution and container permit.
Administer by deep IM injection.
      Name
      Subcutaneous Administration
      Description
      Extended-release once-monthly subcutaneous injection (i.e., Sublocade)
Only a health care professional should prepare and administer this injection.
Approved for use in people who have initiated treatment with a single dose of a transmucosal buprenorphine-containing product or who are already being treated with buprenorphine and are transitioning to this injection for maintenance treatment.
This product should be used as part of a comprehensive treatment program that includes counseling and psychosocial support.
Missed doses: Administer the next dose as soon as possible, with the following dose given no less than 26 days later. Occasional delays in dosing up to 2 weeks are not expected to have a clinically significant impact on treatment effect.
Sublocade injection Administration
Only for subcutaneous injection in the abdomen, thigh, buttock, or back of the upper arm. DO NOT administer intramuscularly, intravenously, or intradermally. Serious harm or death could result if administered intravenously.
Remove Sublocade from the refrigerator prior to administration. The product requires at least 15 minutes to reach room temperature. Do not open the foil pouch until the patient has arrived for his or her injection.
Visually inspect parenteral products for particulate matter and discoloration before administration whenever solution and container permit. Each dose is a clear, colorless to yellow to amber solution.
Administer monthly with a minimum of 26 days between doses.
Only use the syringe and safety needle included with the product for administration. Do not attach the needle until the time of administration.
Rotate injection sites between doses. Do not inject into an area where the skin is irritated, reddened, bruised, infected, or scarred in any way.
Do not rub the injection area after the injection. If bleeding occurs, use a gauze pad or bandage but only use minimal pressure.
Advise the patient that there may be a lump for several weeks after the injection that will decrease in size over time. Instruct the patient not to rub or massage the injection site and to be aware of the placement of any restrictive clothing, such as belts, waistbands, or sleeves.
To avoid irritation, rotate the injection site with each monthly injection.
At the time of the next injection, examine the injection site for infection, evidence of tampering, or attempts to remove the depot.
Storage after removal from refrigeration: Once outside the refrigerator this product may be stored in its original packaging at room temperature, 15 to 30 degrees C (59 to 86 degrees F), for up to 12 weeks prior to administration. Discard the injection if left at room temperature for longer than 12 weeks.
 
Extended-release once-weekly or once-monthly subcutaneous injection (i.e., Brixadi)
Only a health care professional should prepare and administer this injection.
Patients appropriate for Brixadi (weekly) are adults who have tolerated a single 4 mg test dose of a transmucosal buprenorphine-containing product or who are currently receiving treatment with a transmucosal buprenorphine-containing product.
Patients appropriate for Brixadi (monthly) are adults who are currently receiving treatment with a transmucosal buprenorphine-containing product. Do not administer the monthly injection to those who are not currently receiving buprenorphine treatment.
This product should be used as part of a comprehensive treatment program that includes counseling and psychosocial support.
Weekly and monthly doses are different. Doses of the weekly formulation cannot be combined to yield an equivalent monthly dose.
Missed doses: Administer the patient's next dose as soon as possible. Brixadi (weekly) should be administered in 7-day intervals. Brixadi (monthly) should be administered in 28-day intervals. To avoid missed doses, the weekly dose may be administered up to 2 days before or after the weekly time point, and the monthly dose may be administered up to 1 week before or after the monthly time point.
Brixadi injection Administration
Only for slow subcutaneous injection into the buttock, thigh, abdomen, or upper arm. Do NOT administer intravenously, intramuscularly, or intradermally. Serious harm or death could result if administered intravenously.
Remove the safety syringe components from the carton and assemble the safety syringe.
Visually inspect parenteral products for particulate matter and discoloration before administration whenever solution and container permit. The liquid should be clear and yellowish to yellow in color.
Choose an injection site.
Do not administer weekly injections into the same site for at least 8 weeks. Administration of the weekly injection into the upper arm site should only be used after steady-state has been achieved (i.e., following 4 consecutive weekly doses).
No injection site rotation is required for monthly injections.
Administered the injection slowly to the selected area:
Pinch the skin at the injection site between your thumb and index finger. Hold the syringe in your other hand and insert the needle at an angle of approximately 90-degrees. Ensure the needle is fully inserted to enable the medication to reach the subcutaneous space.
Once the needle is fully inserted, release the pinched skin. Slowly press down the plunger head until it latches into the safety device "wings" to ensure all the medication has been injected. Continue to keep the plunger pressed down fully while holding the safety syringe in place for an additional 2 seconds.
Gently pull the needle straight out of the injection site, keeping the plunger fully depressed. Once fully removed from the skin, remove your thumb from the plunger and allow the syringe guard to automatically cover the exposed needle.
Dispose of the safety syringe immediately into an approved sharps container.
Do not rub the injection area after the injection. If bleeding occurs, press a gauze pad or cotton ball on the injection site. An adhesive bandage may be applied if needed.
Patient should be advised to notify their health care professional immediately if excessive swelling, redness, heat, or drainage develops at the injection site.
At the time of the next injection, examine the injection site for infection, evidence of tampering, or attempts to remove the depot.
      Name
      Other Injectable Administration
      Description
      Epidural Administration
NOTE: Buprenorphine is not FDA-approved for epidural administration.
This route of administration should only be used by specially trained health care professionals.
Placement of epidural catheter and administration should be at a site near the dermatomes covering the field of pain to decrease dose requirements and increase specificity. For example, for thoracic surgery placement at T2 to T8, upper abdominal surgery, T4 to L1, lower abdominal surgery, T10 to L3, upper extremity surgery, C2 to C8 and lower extremity surgery, T12 to L3.
After ensuring proper placement of the needle or catheter, inject appropriate dose into the epidural space.
    Name
    Injectable Administration
    Administration Details
      Name
      Transdermal Patch Formulations
      Description
      Transdermal patch system (e.g., Butrans)
Apply to intact skin only.
Do not use if the pouch seal is broken or the patch is cut, damaged, or changed in any way. Do not cut the buprenorphine transdermal system.
Instruct patients to apply immediately after removal from the individually sealed pouch.
Each buprenorphine transdermal system is intended to be worn for 7 days.
Apply to the upper outer arm, upper chest, upper back, or the side of the chest. Rotate buprenorphine transdermal system application among the 8 described sites (each present on both sides of the body). After removal of the patch, wait a minimum of 21 days before reapplying to the same skin site.
Apply to a hairless or nearly hairless skin site. If necessary, hair should be clipped, not shaven. Do not apply to irritated skin.
If necessary, clean site with water only and allow to dry completely before application. Do not use soaps, alcohol, oils, lotions, or abrasive devices.
The patch may be worn while bathing or showering.
Avoid exposing the patch application site and surrounding areas to external heat sources (e.g., heating pads, electric blankets, heated water beds, hair dryers, tanning beds, hot baths or saunas, excessive sun exposure, or hot climate).
If the adhesive matrix (which contains buprenorphine) of the patch accidentally touches skin, wash the area with water. Do not use soap, alcohol, or other solvents to remove the adhesive; doing so may enhance drug absorption.
If problems arise with the adherence of the patch, the edges of the patch may be taped with first aid tape. If problems with the patch not sticking continue, cover the patch with special see-through adhesive dressings (e.g., Bioclusive or Tegaderm).
If the buprenorphine transdermal system falls off during the 7 day dosing interval, dispose of the patch properly and place a new patch at a different skin site.
Storage: Keep buprenorphine secured in a location not accessible by others.
Disposal: Dispose of damaged, used, and unneeded buprenorphine patches right away by folding patch in half so that the adhesive side is inward and immediately flush down the toilet. Alternatively, buprenorphine patches may be sealed in the Patch-Disposal Unit provided and then disposed of in the trash. Never dispose of a buprenorphine patch in the trash without sealing it in the Patch-Disposal Unit.

    Topical Administration

Dosage Adjustment Guidelines
  Hepatic Impairment
  Immediate-release parenteral dosage forms (e.g., Buprenex or generic equivalent):
Specific guidelines for dosage adjustments in hepatic impairment are not available; however, dosage adjustments may be necessary. Adjust based on clinical response.
 
Extended-release subcutaneous injection (e.g., Sublocade, Brixadi, or generic equivalents):
Moderate to severe hepatic impairment (e.g., Child Pugh B or C): Use is not recommended.
Mild hepatic impairment (e.g., Child Pugh A): No dosage adjustments are needed.
 
Sublingual dosage form (e.g., Subutex or generic equivalents):
Severe hepatic impairment: Consider reducing the starting and titration incremental dose by half, and monitor for signs and symptoms of toxicity or overdose caused by increased levels of buprenorphine.
Moderate hepatic impairment: No dosage adjustment is needed; however, patients should be monitored for signs and symptoms of toxicity or overdose caused by increased levels of buprenorphine.
Mild hepatic impairment: No dosage adjustment or specific monitoring is required.
 
Transdermal patch (e.g., Butrans):
In patients with mild to moderate hepatic impairment where the patch is the first opioid analgesic, initiate patients on 5 mcg/hour transdermally. Otherwise, cautiously select a dose that corresponds to the patient's oral morphine equivalents per day and other factors that influence initial patch selection (see manufacturer's literature). In some patients, alternate therapy should be considered. Closely monitor the patient for the first 24 to 72 hours of treatment with the patch for respiratory depression. Individually titrate the dose thereafter to a level that provides adequate analgesia and tolerable side effects. The buprenorphine transdermal system has not been evaluated in patients with severe hepatic impairment and is only intended for 7-day application. Consider the use of an alternate analgesic with more flexibility of dosing in patients with severe hepatic impairment.
 
Buccal film (e.g., Belbuca):
Severe hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh C): Reduce starting dose and titration dose by half that of patients with normal liver function, from 150 mcg to 75 mcg.
Mild to moderate hepatic impairment: No dose adjustment necessary.
  Renal Impairment
  Specific guidelines for dosage adjustments in renal impairment are not available; it appears no dosage adjustments are needed. Some manufacturers recommend cautious use in severe renal impairment.
